to Russian state-controlled media amid EU states’ call for action,” Euractiv, March 1, 2022,\nhttps://www.euractiv.com/section/digital/news/youtube-blocks-access-to-…\n.\nB3\n1.00-4.00 pts\n0-4 pts\nDo restrictions on the internet and digital content lack transparency, proportionality to the stated aims, or an independent appeals process?\n3.00\n\n3\n4.00\n4\nRestrictions on online content in Germany generally meet minimum requirements for transparency, proportionality, and independent appeal.\nNetzDG, which went into effect in January 2018, obliges social media platforms with more than two million registered users in the country to investigate and delete flagged content shortly after being reported, or otherwise face hefty fines (see B6).\n1\nIf the flagged content is “obviously illegal,” the company must block or remove it within 24 hours; if not obviously illegal, the content must be investigated and blocked or removed within seven days. Under NetzDG, illegality is defined in relation to 22 articles in Germany’s criminal code (see C2).\n2\nAfter deciding to delete or preserve flagged content, the company has to inform both the complainant and the user who uploaded the content. If it fails to meet any of those requirements, the company could face fines of up to €50 million ($51.8 million) (see B6).\n3\nAdditionally, June 2021 amendments to NetzDG add extra transparency requirements for social media platforms and expand the mandate of NetzDG to include video-sharing services to align with the EU Audiovisual Media Services Directive. The amendments also provide users with an avenue to appeal content-removal decisions, though the appeal must be filed within two weeks.\n4\nAn amendment to NetzDG passed in June 2020 required companies to share with the Federal Criminal Police Office (BKA) the personal information of users reported for hate speech or illegal content (see C6). The Cologne Administrative Court in March 2022 ruled that measures in the amendments were not in line with EU legislation.\n5\nIn April 2023, the Ministry of Justice announced plans to propose a law on “digital violence” (“Gesetz gegen digitale Gewalt”), which is broadly defined. The proposed law would compel platforms to block users who engage in online harassment and, in some cases, provide law enforcement and victims with the users’ internet protocol (IP) addresses. According to the announcement, courts could order platforms to block users who repeatedly perpetrate such harassment (see C2 and C4).\n6\nThe EU’s DSA, which will come into force in February 2024 (see B2), will regulate online and illegal speech across the EU, and will supersede parts of NetzDG in Germany. Politicians and children’s rights groups have advocated for the continued enforcement of the stricter NetzDG, which has a broader definition for illegal content and compels online platforms to remove a wider swath of content than the DSA, even after the DSA passed.\n7\nFor example, the nongovernmental organization (NGO) National Coalition Germany, a network of children’s rights groups, put forward a letter demanding that the stricter German youth protection laws should be allowed to remain in place.\n8\nIn March 2021, major internet providers operating in Germany and associations of companies in the entertainment industry formed the Clearing House for Copyright on the Internet (CUII), a joint initiative to initiate DNS blocks against “structurally copyright-infringing” websites.\n9\nWhile blocking requests were previously considered by judges, the BNetzA now assesses CUII recommendations and determines whether the blocking violates the principle of net neutrality.\nThe new policy has elicited criticism from politicians and activists.\n10\n\nCritics question the BNetzA’s suitability for this role, since the agency is responsible for regulating telecommunications networks to ensure fair competition and net neutrality rather than assessing fundamental rights regarding copyright.\n11\nThere have also been concerns about normalizing this type of instrument and the initialization of the DNS4EU project, which will also operate with filter lists and network blocks across the EU, demonstrates that similar measures could take effect at the European level.\n12\nThe Federal Cartel Office (BKartA) did not object to the new initiative, though it is monitoring the development of the practice.\n13\nIssues related to copyright law have figured prominently in discussions around internet freedom in Germany. The EU Directive on Copyright in the Digital Single Market, which the Council of the EU approved in April 2019\n14\nand Germany enacted in June 2021 (see B2), imposes a so-called link tax, which grants online publishers the right to charge aggregators like Google News for excerpting proprietary content, such as news articles (see B6).\n15\nIn May 2021, the federal and state parliaments passed laws amending the Copyright Act and the Collecting Societies Act to align with the new EU directive (see B2). The amendments require companies with large amounts of user-generated content to use “upload filters,”\n16\nwhich will preemptively block copyright-infringing online content.\n17\nThe law came into effect in June 2021 but the implementation of the upload filters was narrowed by an ECJ judgment in April 2022, which stipulated that states must build in safeguards that protect fundamental rights so legal uploads are not blocked.\n18\nUpload filters cannot reliably make this distinction.\nCompanies can be held liable for illegal content under the Telemedia Act (TMG). The law distinguishes between full liability for one’s own content and limited “breach of duty of care” (\nstörerhaftung\n) for service providers and host providers of third-party content.\n19\nThe courts have held that if the business model of a service aims to facilitate copyright infringement, the company is considered less worthy of immunity from intermediary liability.\n20\nAs a consequence, hosting providers are required to monitor their own servers and search for copyright-protected content as soon as they have been notified of a possible violation.\n21", "excerpt_sha256": "d897907ab588f1bdaa1f53640ae21fdd061f493012532b7792512fe168d26844", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0010-E002", "source_id": "SRC-bb0a509f84180c43", "url": "https://freedomhouse.org/country/germany/freedom-net/2024", "title": "Germany: Freedom on the Net 2024 Country Report", "publisher": "Freedom House", "raw_sha256": "311e1b42a7a16fa2c4f17bc1bde5ed207530a183746c600ece7af9f6da1ceab0", "fact_summary": "The Federal Network Agency was criticized for a politically concentrated governance structure; in winter 2023 the federal government strengthened its independence and powers, although opposition parties criticized the resulting 'super regulator' as insufficiently checked.", "question_ids": ["4B.4", "5B.7", "8B.2", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly relevant to 2023; the report also describes institutional history before 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "Federal government under Scholz's coalition; personal attribution to Scholz is not established.", "limitations": ["The finding concerns an agency and government action, not a personal decision by Scholz.", "Opposition criticism is reported but not independently adjudicated here."], "start_unit": 13, "end_unit": 14, "locator": "extracted HTML block 13 through extracted HTML block 14", "exact_excerpt": "Dialog Consult and VATM, “25. As Digital Service Coordinator, the BNetzA will also be the central complaints office for citizens and the point of contact for the appointment and coordination of trusted flaggers, or organizations and individuals who are selected to reliably identify content that requires actions.\n5\nIn September 2021, the European Court of Justice (ECJ)—the EU’s supreme court and a part of the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU)—upheld a complaint by the EC, ruling that the BNetzA would need to become more independent.\n6\nIn the winter of 2023, the federal government responded to the complaint by strengthening the authority's independence and increasing its powers. The BNetzA is now responsible for determining the conditions and modalities for access to the electricity and gas grids nationwide.\n7\nThe agency's new autonomy, however, has been criticized by the opposition, who argue that it is a “super regulator” without adequate checks and balances.\n8\n1\nMarkus Beckedahl, “Verkehrsministerium gewinnt Fachaufsicht über Bundesnetzagentur”[Ministry of Transport gains supervision over Federal Network Agency],“ Netzpolitik, February 14, 2014,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2014/verkehrsministerium-gewinnt-fachaufsicht-u…\n.\n2\nBundesministerium der Justiz und für Verbraucherschutz, “Gesetz über die Bundesnetzagentur für Elektrizität, Gas, Telekommunikation, Post und Eisenbahnen [Law on the Federal Network Agency for Electricity, Gas, Telecommunications, Post and Railways],“ §3, Abs. 3 BEGTPG. Judicial bodies operate independently and generally support the protection of basic rights.\nSince 2016, the Office of the Federal Commissioner for Data Protection and Freedom of Information (BfDI) has been an independent supreme federal authority.\n1\nSince its founding, the agency has tripled its capacities and enlarged its staff to strengthen the supervision of security authorities.\n2\nCommissioners are appointed by the government for five-year terms. During the coverage period, critics expressed concern about a lack of transparency in the selection process and how that could affect the authority's independence.”\nThe controversial Federal Intelligence Service (BND) Law (see C5), which expands the legal justification for surveillance, has been criticized by journalists demanding improvements, including better protection for reporters abroad as well as their sources. In May 2020, the Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) found the law to be unconstitutional, forcing the government to revise it. Reporters Without Borders (RSF) launched a campaign against the new draft law under the hashtag #NotYourSource, arguing that the vague wording of the revised paragraphs still poses risks to journalists. The amendment was approved by the Bundestag and Bundesrat in March 2021 and went into effect in January 2022.\n\n3\nIn January 2023, Reporters Without Borders (RSF) filed a new complaint to the Federal Constitutional Court, saying protections for journalists remained too weak.\n4\nThe complaint was rejected in February 2024 due to insufficient reasoning.\n5\nCivil society groups have also criticized measures in the EMFA (see B6) that permits authorities to target journalists with spyware,\n6\nthough the act does place safeguards on those cases.\n1\n“Endlich! Unabhängige Datenschutzbehörde für Deutschland [Finally! most state ministries said they would refrain from comparable software, while Bavaria, North Rhine-Westphalia, and Hesse intended to continue using it or evaluate continued use.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "3B.5", "4B.4", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly relevant to 2023, with implementation positions reported through the end of the coverage period.", "ruler_attribution": "The finding concerns the independent constitutional court and state governments, not Scholz's personal action.", "limitations": ["The federal chancellor lacks direct authority over the cited state deployments.", "The report does not establish whether federal government action influenced the state responses."], "start_unit": 67, "end_unit": 68, "locator": "extracted HTML block 67 through extracted HTML block 68", "exact_excerpt": "and, in January 2018, several nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) and foreign journalists filed a constitutional complaint.\n13\nIn May 2020, the Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) declared that the law was unconstitutional in response to the complaint. The BVerfG ruled that the law violated fundamental rights, and the German government's obligation to respect fundamental rights is not limited to German territory. Therefore, the government had to revise the law.\n14\nIn January 2022, an amended version of the BND Law took effect.\n15\nThe law expands the scope of BND activities and allows the interception of up to 30 percent of the transmission capacity of all global telecommunications networks.\n16\nMoreover, the legislation permits the BND to conduct online searches of foreigners abroad in addition to the previously permitted source telecommunication surveillance. While the law protects “individual communications of natural persons,” it allows the BND to collect and process various kinds of information, including inventory, traffic, and content data, enabling the monitoring of communications behavior, financial transaction data, and movement of individuals in Germany and abroad. The insufficient protection of journalists, criticized by the BVerfG, is regulated in an added paragraph. However, the Federal Data Protection Commissioner, opposition politicians, and legal observers have criticized vague exceptions in the protection clause.\n17\nIn January 2023, RSF filed a new complaint to the BVerfG, saying protections for journalists remained too weak.\n18\nHowever, this constitutional complaint was rejected in February 2024, due to insufficient reasoning.\n19\nTelecommunications interception by state authorities for criminal prosecutions is regulated by the criminal code and may only be employed for the prosecution of serious crimes for which specific evidence exists and when other, less intrusive investigative methods are likely to fail. In August 2017, the federal parliament enacted the “Law for More Effective and More Practical Criminal Proceedings.” An extensive list of criminal offenses allows police and investigative authorities to intercept phone calls. The law expands the scope of the list to allow the use of remote forensic software on suspects’ mobile phones, tablets, and computers to enable monitoring of written and spoken text and the copying of data.\n20\nMoreover, the legislation delineates specific criminal offenses that permit the use of source telecommunication surveillance and online searches. The use of remote forensic software for criminal prosecution is regulated by this law, while the law allows the states to regulate preventive hacking in their respective police laws.\n21\nIn line with legislation, several state laws have been enacted that grant police vastly expanded powers to access communications.\n22\nCritics consider the 2017 legislation unconstitutional due to its expansive scope and long list of applicable offenses, claiming it contradicts the 2008 BVerfG ruling on Article 2 of the Basic Law.\n23\nAt the national level, the Society for Civil Rights (GFF) filed a constitutional complaint against the law in 2018.\n24\nIn April 2023, GFF was given the opportunity to make representations in the proceedings.\n\n25\nNevertheless, the BVerfG had not reached a verdict as of the end of the coverage period.\n26\nThe constitutional complaint against the police law in Baden-Württemberg was rejected in 2021; in its reasoning, the BVerfG derived a state duty to protect the information technology (IT) security of all Germans. Therefore, the state must weigh the exploitation of security flaws in its deployment of remote forensic software against the information security interests of the public.\n27\nIn 2022, 4,981 telecommunication interceptions were ordered, most frequently for drug crimes.\n28\nIn addition, the surveillance of IT devices by means of the remote forensic software and source telecommunications surveillance, was ordered in 94 proceedings and carried out in 49 cases.\n29\nFinally, six online searches were ordered, of which four were actually carried out.\n30\nIn comparison, in 2021, 5,174 telecommunication interceptions, 23 instances of source telecommunications surveillance and 9 online searches were conducted.\n31\nIn July 2023, the Ministry of Justice presented a bill to place some restrictions on the use of remote forensic software for police authorities. Currently, 44 different crimes permit the use of source telecommunication surveillance and 33 particularly serious crimes permit the use of online searches. With the amendment, the 33 crimes will apply to both the use of source telecommunication surveillance and online searches. In addition, the secret activation of cameras and microphones will be explicitly prohibited in the amendment.\n32\nIn Hesse in 2018, legislation establishing law enforcement’s right to use remote forensic software also allowed agencies to conduct automated analysis of personal data. Hesse uses Gotham, a predictive policing tool produced by the US–based company Palantir to analyze different data sources, including internal police databases and Facebook data.\n33\nNorth Rhine–Westphalia purchased similar software in 2020, Bavaria in 2022, and Hamburg reportedly planned to implement it in 2019.\n34\nThese tools have been criticized for using discriminatory data bases and producing mass errors.\n35\nIn February 2023, the BVerfG ruled that bills in Hesse and Hamburg that legalized data mining and predictive policing were unconstitutional.\n36\nFollowing the ruling, most federal states’ ministries have declared their intention to refrain from utilizing Gotham or analogous software in the future. Nevertheless, Bavaria, North Rhine–Westphalia, and Hesse have indicated that they intend to continue using the software, while Berlin, Hamburg, and Thuringia were evaluating its potential deployment as of the end of the coverage period.\n37", "excerpt_sha256": "9239ce4080805d5aebed6df8fe19fc09e4a0825abd4f515c703dc4313c3ab8f1", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports the February 2023 constitutional ruling and the reported positions of the state ministries."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R01-E010", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "As of December 2023, BAMF had 8,447 positions, including 7,355 full-time positions in various asylum departments, and maintained quality-management, operational-management, and special-security functions. As of February 2024, the BAMF website lists a total\nof 58 branch offices.33 The branch offices process the asylum procedures, but also carry out additional\ntasks (for instance, they function as contact points for authorities and organisations active in the\nintegration of foreign nationals, while some branch offices work exclusively on Dublin cases). Branch\noffices are assigned specific countries of origin, whereas the main countries of origin are processed in the\n\n\n\n33\n        BAMF, Locations, available at: https://bit.ly/3dFTd8w.\n\n\n                                                          23\n\nmajority of branch offices.34 In cooperation with the Federal States, the BAMF manages a distribution\nsystem for asylum seekers known as Initial Distribution of Asylum Seekers (Erstverteilung der\nAsylbegehrenden, EASY) system, which allocates places according to a quota system known as\n“Königsteiner Schlüssel” (see Asylum Act). The quota is based on the size and the economic strength of\nthe Federal States in which the centres are located. Furthermore, the system takes into account which\nbranch office of the BAMF deals with an asylum seeker's country of origin.\n\nAs of November 2023, the BAMF had 7.355 positions or “full-time job equivalents” working on various\naspects of asylum (meaning that the actual number of staff is much higher, since many of these positions\nare shared by people working part-time). Since the office is responsible for several other tasks on top of\nthe asylum procedure (e.g., research, integration, migration for reasons other than asylum and return\npolicies), not all staff members are working in the area of asylum. The overall number of positions is 8,138\naccording to the Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Community.35\n\nThe government provided the following numbers for positions in the relevant departments as of November\n2023:36\n        v asylum department (excluding revocation and Dublin procedures): 2,327.9 full-time\n          equivalents;\n        v revocation procedures: 105.2 full-time equivalents;\n        v procedures (appeal procedures, representation of the BAMF in court): 376.0 full-time\n          equivalents;\n        v quality management: 141.0 full-time equivalents;\n        v Dublin-procedures: 338.4 full-time equivalents.\n\nThe BAMF also has special officers for security issues. They are responsible for a whole range of issues\nand should be involved in asylum procedures whenever indications arise for instances of ‘extremism,\nterrorism, criminality, human trafficking, war crimes, crimes against humanity and smuggling of human\nbeings’.37 The special officers for security issues act as contact points between the BAMF and other\nauthorities, but they do not necessarily take part in interviews or take over responsibility of particular\nasylum procedures.\n\nQuality\n\nThe quality of BAMF asylum decisions has been much debated in recent years given the high number of\nappeals filed at the courts, but also because of “scandals” which prompted extensive media coverage in\n2017 and 2018.38 This was related, in part, to the high increase in personnel in 2015 and 2016 – likely\ndue to the spike in asylum applications –, accompanied by shortened training phases, with some decision-\nmakers not having received relevant training. As a result, the BAMF has undertaken several changes to\nthe training provided to decision-makers and to the quality assurance procedures since 2017. As of 2018,\nshort summaries of interview transcripts and notice letters are checked by a second employee. Randomly\nselected cases are subject to a more thorough quality control by the BAMF’s quality assurance division.\nIn addition, the BAMF also has a division for ‘Operative management of asylum procedures and\nintegration’ which ‘analyses developments and trends so that it is possible to recognise and react to a\nneed to act for management at an early date’, according to the BAMF.39 In particular, the decision-making\npractices of the different branch offices are monitored and branch offices with significant deviations from\n\n34\n      A list of all countries of origin and the allocated branch offices is available on the website of the Refugee\n      Council of Lower Saxony (up to date as of March 20221): https://bit.ly/3WJ0eg1.\n35\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community, ‘Bundesamt für Migration und Flüchtlinge’, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/3qTH0qt.\n36\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/9933, 28 December 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/42NJDMa, 30-31.\n37\n      BAMF, Entscheiderbrief (newsletter for decision-makers) 4/2020, 1.\n38\n      For more detailed information, see AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2019, July 2020,\n      available at: https://bit.ly/34so09M, 20-21.\n39\n      BAMF, Procedure management and quality assurance, 28 November 2018, available at: http://bit.ly/3DxsTgJ.\n\n\n                                                        24", "excerpt_sha256": "8e89c9fc9799b9e48de2bb9d933ba0310ce8d043d23aba81d6af8ac40819ba38", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not support the claim that monitoring results were not automatically public."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R03-E002", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; NGOs and a federal working group continued to report that identification was not systematic, and the federal government did not systematically monitor implementation by the Länder.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "6B.3", "6B.5", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The institutional finding and 2023 criticism apply during the target year, although some reforms predate 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The federal government and BAMF established the guidance and monitoring framework; Additionally, since\n2022 the BAMF internal guidelines lay down the standards for the BAMF employees to identify\nvulnerabilities in order to guarantee a fair asylum procedure for the persons concerned.526 These\nguidelines are updated regularly and are to be used conjointly with the concept mentioned above. A 2016\namendment to the German Asylum Act introduced wording relevant to the identification of vulnerable\nasylum seekers by allowing Federal States to transmit personal information about an applicant’s\nvulnerabilities to the BAMF. In turn, the BAMF has the obligation to transmit relevant information on\nvulnerabilities to the Federal States if they are necessary for adequate accommodation.527\nHowever, lacking clear duties of identification, the Asylum Act still fails to properly transpose the recast\nAPD, as it only requires the BAMF to ‘duly carry out’ the interview and not to provide ‘adequate support’\nto applicants in need of special procedural guarantees throughout the duration of the procedure.528 In\npractice, therefore, identification procedures in Germany have been described as ‘a matter of luck and\ncoincidence’, given that authorities ‘are not able to systematically undertake the necessary steps to\nascertain mental disorders or trauma.’529\n\nPrior to the revision of the law on counselling in 2023, the BAMF stated that the counselling service for\nasylum-seekers, consisting of general information on the procedure as well as the opportunity to make\nindividual appointments with BAMF staff (see Information for asylum seekers and access to NGOs and\nUNHCR) has led to vulnerabilities ‘being partially identified more often’ as counsellors inform applicants\nabout rights of vulnerable applicants during the procedure. As of 1 January 2023, the BAMF provides\nadditional funding for independent counsellors providing support for vulnerable groups.530 The\nindependent counsellors are required to transmit personal information to the BAMF and the Federal States\nagencies responsible for the accommodation of asylum seekers which is relevant for the identification\nand support of vulnerable groups, if the applicant consents.531 However, no details were given concerning\nthe number or the type of vulnerabilities which were identified in the course of the new advice service.\nAccording to information provided by the BAMF, no data are collected on vulnerabilities detected during\nthe counselling nor on the number of vulnerable persons applying for asylum in Germany.532 The BAMF\naffirms that the funding for independent counsellors is dependent on relevant certifications and\n\n\n\n523\n      BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. In 2023, the Federal working group on psycho-social\ncentres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs acknowledged that there\nhave been attempts mainly by the Federal States to address these shortcomings. However, the working\ngroup repeated the criticism of no systematic approach.536 Along with these policy demands, the\nassociations introduced a toolbox on vulnerabilities which provides guidance for counselling and the\nidentification of vulnerabilities.537\n\nThe procedures and practice of identification in reception centres, which are run by the Federal States,\nvary. Upon initial registration, all asylum seekers should undergo a medical examination, which usually\ntakes place shortly after the registration of the asylum application in the arrival centre. However, this\nexamination is focused on the detection of communicable diseases and does not include a screening for\npotential vulnerabilities. Sometimes medical personnel or other staff members working in the reception\ncentres inform the BAMF if they recognise symptoms of trauma, the BAMF provides notification sheets\nwith which vulnerabilities can be communicated but there is no systematic procedure in place ensuring\nthat such information is passed on.538\n\nAs of 2020, only three Federal States (Berlin, Brandenburg and Lower Saxony) had a structured\nprocedure in place to identify particularly vulnerable asylum seekers.539 A number of States conduct\nscreenings, offer psychiatric or psychological consultations or refer to the general care infrastructure, and\nsome Federal States have integrated identification in their concepts for protection from violence in\nreception centres (Hamburg, Hesse, North Rhine Westphalia, Rhineland-Palatinate, Saarland,\nSaxony, Schleswig-Holstein).540 Since 2021, a project led by the Federal working group on psycho-\nsocial centres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs as well as the BAMF\nand local authorities has developed a concept to identify vulnerable applicants in reception centres and\nin psycho-social centres. The concept was piloted in two reception centres in North Rhine Westphalia and\nBremen and the findings were published in March 2023 in a toolbox of guidelines which provide guidance\nfor counselling and the identification of vulnerabilities.541 The working group continues its work, and the\nFederal government affirms that it supports those Federal States who are interested in implementing the\n\n\n\n\n533\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC.\n534\n      KOK, Betroffene von Menschenhandel im Asylkontext erkennen. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin. Status quo in den Bundesländern, Modelle und Herausforderungen’, June 2020,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/3GsdrSm, 26.\n550\n      See e.g.: Refugee Council Berlin, Kein Ort für Schutzsuchende: Notunterkunft im Flughafen Tegel schließen,\n      13 March 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Ufa7Um.\n\n\n                                                         94\n\nthe Berlin senate in 2022,551 the Berlin Refugee Council criticises that this pre-screening mechanism has\nnever been implemented and that instead no specified reception conditions or other support mechanisms\nfor asylum seekers with special needs are in place in certain arrival centres.552\n\nIn Brandenburg, a questionnaire is handed out upon registration in the initial reception centre to detect\nvulnerabilities and possible psychological disorders. If the questionnaire indicates a potential vulnerability,\na screening interview takes place with the socio-psychological service of the Brandenburg immigration\nauthority (Zentrale Ausländerbehörde). Following the screening interview, if a vulnerability is detected\napplicants are referred to psychiatric counselling (which only takes place in Eisenhüttenstadt) and can be\nhoused in a special house for vulnerable applicants. The vulnerability is also communicated to the BAMF.\nHowever, in 2016 this was the case for under 1% of all asylum seekers, indicating that detection rates are\nvery low compared to the estimated prevalence of psychological distress among asylum seekers.\nFurthermore, the special accommodation houses both single men with psychological difficulties and single\nwomen who might have been victims of sexual violence.553 The Brandenburg Refugee Council criticises\nthat while there is a coordinated approach to identify vulnerabilities, the support measures vary depending\non the local governance. Accordingly, in some regions no specialised services for the accommodation of\nasylum seekers with special needs are available.554\n\nIn Lower Saxony, projects to improve the identification of vulnerable groups have been established in\nreception centres first in Friedland in 2012 and have since then expanded to all reception centres in the\nFederal State.555 Upon registration, all applicants are informed about special vulnerabilities during a\nmeeting with the social service of the reception centre and a further diagnosis is carried out in cases\nwhere there are indications of psychological disorders. While the authorities usually follow the\nrecommendations which follow from the diagnosis, between 2015 and 2017 only very few people were\nreferred for a diagnosis.556\n\nIn e.g., Rhineland-Palatinate,557 the regional government has adopted a protection concept which also\nincludes methods for the identification of vulnerabilities.558 This includes the following measures:\n     v Obligation to check for possible vulnerabilities in the reception centres during the initial stages of\n         the reception process and the asylum procedure;\n     v Intensification of communication between various actors and authorities involved in the reception\n         system and in the first steps of the asylum procedure;\n     v Documentation of possible vulnerabilities in a data system used by all authorities involved in the\n         reception process and in the asylum procedure;\n     v Training measures for persons employed by the Federal State in the reception centres to raise\n         awareness on the different forms of vulnerabilities.\n\n551\n      Berlin Senate, Senat beschließt Verfahren zur Unterstützung für besonders schutzbedürftige Geflüchtete aus\n      der Ukraine, 5 April 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48NlJCt.\n552\n      Refugee Council Berlin, Kein Ort für Schutzsuchende: Notunterkunft im Flughafen Tegel schließen, 13 March\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Ufa7Um.\n553\n      See BafF, ‘Identifizierung besonderer Schutzbedürftigkeit am Beispiel von Personen mit\n      Traumafolgestörungen. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "By June 2023, BAMF had 1,267 staff members serving as special officers for vulnerable applicants; their roles covered unaccompanied children, gender-specific persecution, trauma or torture, and trafficking, with 410 officers qualified for unaccompanied-children cases.", "question_ids": ["3B.2", "3B.7", "4B.5", "6B.4", "8B.4"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly concerns staffing and institutional capacity in June 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "BAMF recruited, trained, and deployed the special officers under the federal government during Scholz's chancellorship.", "limitations": ["Some officers held multiple specialisations, so the category totals cannot be summed.", "The source does not show whether staffing was sufficient for workload or whether outcomes improved nationwide."], "start_unit": 98, "end_unit": 99, "locator": "page 98 through page 99", "exact_excerpt": "Moreover, young persons usually lose any entitlement to be supported in legal matters by the youth\nauthorities once they are declared to be adults in the course of the age assessment.574\n\nLatest numbers from 2021 show that 3,999 unaccompanied minors have been taken into custody. For\n1,608 of those this measure was ended due to an age assessment.575\n\n      2. Special procedural guarantees\n\n                                    Indicators: Special Procedural Guarantees\n\n      1. Are there special procedural arrangements/guarantees for vulnerable people?\n                                                           Yes        For certain categories      No\n             v If for certain categories, specify which: Unaccompanied children, traumatised persons\n                                                         Victims of torture or violence\n\n         2.1. Adequate support during the interview\n\nThe BAMF does not have specialised units dealing with vulnerable groups. According to the BAMF, all\ncase workers complete the EUAA training module on ‘Interviewing Vulnerable Persons’.576 If an applicant\nor a Federal State authority submits information to the BAMF that indicates vulnerability (such as medical\nrecords or information about specific physical, mental, intellectual or sensory impairments), such\ninformation is transferred to the case worker in charge who can decide to take measures such as allocate\nmore time for the interview, appoint an interpreter of a specific gender or allow the person to bring a\ntrusted person of their choice to the interview.577\n\nFor specific groups of vulnerable persons, the BAMF employs ‘special officers’ (Sonderbeauftragte)\nresponsible for interviews and decisions on claims by applicants with special needs. Special officers also\nadvise their colleagues in dealing with vulnerable applicants and are contact persons for specialised\ncounselling services and psycho-social centres.578 Staff members who become special officers must\ncomplete a training module for the specialisation they want to achieve. In addition, they follow the EASO\ntraining modules for their specialisation.579 Training covers both the identification and in the treatment of\nvulnerable persons.580 According to the BAMF, continuous training is offered for specific topics in the realm\nof the special officers’ responsibilities.581 The BAMF guidelines stipulate that the following cases shall be\nhandled in a particularly sensitive manner and, if necessary, by specially-trained decision-makers:582\n     v Unaccompanied children;\n     v Victims of gender-specific prosecution;\n     v Victims of human trafficking; and\n     v Victims of torture and traumatised asylum seekers.\n\nAs of June 2023, a total of 1,267 BAMF had one or more roles as special officers. This corresponds to\nroughly a third of full-time equivalent positions allocated to the first instance procedure (see Number of\n\n574\n        Nerea González Méndez de Vigo, Alterseinschätzung – ein Irrgarten ohne Ausweg? Rechtlicher Rahmen und\n        Verfahren der Alterseinschätzung in Deutschland, Asylmagazin 6-7/2019, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/2VI0w9G, 206-217.\n575\n        Statistisches Bundesamt, Statistiken der Kinder- und Jugendhilfe, Vorläufige Schutzmaßnahmen 2021, 27\n        July 2022, avialable in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sz6mru, 28.\n576\n        BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Umsetzung in der Praxis des Bundesamtes\n        für Migration und Flüchtlinge, June 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Z7JHCU, 16.\n577\n        BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Umsetzung in der Praxis des Bundesamtes\n        für Migration und Flüchtlinge, June 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Z7JHCU, 19.\n578\n        Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023.\n579\n        BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Umsetzung in der Praxis des Bundesamtes\n        für Migration und Flüchtlinge, June 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Z7JHCU, 16.\n580\n        BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Umsetzung in der Praxis des Bundesamtes\n        für Migration und Flüchtlinge, June 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Z7JHCU, 16.\n581\n        Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023.\n582\n        BAMF, DA-Asyl (Dienstanweisung Asylverfahren) – Belehrungen, 2010, 139.\n\n\n                                                       98\n\nstaff and nature of the first instance authority). The distribution among areas of responsibilities was the\nfollowing: Unaccompanied children (410), victims of gender-specific persecution (312) traumatised\npersons and victims of torture (291), victims of trafficking (254).583\n\nSpecially trained case officers may be included at all times of the asylum procedure, or take over, also\nprior to the interview, if vulnerabilitites are known. For example, if it becomes evident during the interview\nthat an asylum seeker belongs to one of these groups, the officer conducting the interview is obliged to\nconsult a special officer, in addition to notifying the reception centre if necessary and authorised by the\napplicant.584 A note on how the officers are planning to proceed must be added by the special officer to\nthe file, particularly if the special officer takes over the case as a result of their consultation. According to\ninformation provided by the government, there is an obligation in cases of unaccompanied minors for\nspecial officers to take over responsibility for the asylum procedures. In other cases of other vulnerable\ngroups, the specially trained case officer must be consulted and there are two options for further\nprocedures: either the special officer adopts an advisory role or they take over responsibility for the\nprocedure.585 However, the BAMF does not record the number of cases in which special officers are\nconsulted or in which procedures are handed over to special officers.586\n\nLawyers have reported that the introduction of special officers has led to some improvement in the\nhandling of ‘sensitive’ cases. The special officers receive special training in the area of LGBTIQ, they\nconduct interviews upon consultation by other BAMF officers and serve as multipliers for their\ncolleagues.587However, there have also been examples of cases in which indications of trauma and even\nexplicit references to torture did not lead to special officers being consulted.588 It has further been criticised\nthat there are shorcomings in the effective implementation of procedural guarantees for LGBTIQ+\npersons, which increases the risk of false decisions.589 There is no individual right to have a special officer\nhandling a person’s case, except for unaccompanied minors for whom this is mandatory. But if evidence\nsuggests that the person is vulnerable or if the person claims to have certain vulnerabilities, the interviewer\nis required to involve a special officer in the procedure, e.g. as consultant.590 However, the Administrative\nCourt of Berlin ruled that if special vulnerabilities have been detected, the absence of a special officer in\nthe asylum procedure constitutes a violation of procedural rights of vulnerable asylum seekers.591\n\n        2.2. Exemption from special procedures\n\nThe German Asylum Act exempts neither unaccompanied children nor persons with special procedural\nguarantees from the airport procedure, despite an express obligation under the recast APD to provide for\nsuch exemptions under certain conditions.592 It also makes no reference to ‘adequate support’ which\nshould be provided to those requiring special procedural guarantees.593\n\n\n\n\n583\n       The government notes that the figures cannot be added since some officers may have qualified in more than\n       one area; Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/7503, 28 June 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sb12cF, 2-4.\n584\n       BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. It is only ‘in special circumstances and\nindividual cases’ that further benefits can be granted on a discretionary basis.883 It has been estimated\nthat this may result in a reduction of almost 50% of the benefits in many cases.884 Benefits covering the\npersonal needs of everyday life (‘pocket money’) can be withdrawn entirely. Furthermore, asylum seekers\nare not entitled to benefits covering the costs of clothing and for ‘durable and non-durable consumer\ngoods for the household’. Clothes and household goods can only be provided ‘in kind’ and on an ad hoc\nbasis, if necessary, but these costs are not included in the monthly benefits for the persons concerned.885\n\nThe authorities are required to limit the reduction of benefits to a 6 months period. After this time, the\ndecision to reduce benefits has to be reviewed and can only be extended if the ground for reduction is\nstill applicable.886 Even before the end of the 6-months time limit, benefits have to be restored to the\nstandard level if the legal prerequisites for the reduction cease to apply. If benefits are reduced following\na rejection of an application, they can be restored to the standard level at a later stage, e.g., if a\nsubsequent application leads to the opening of a new asylum procedure, or if it turns out that a removal\nis not possible for reasons which cannot be held against the concerned person.\n\nThe decision to reduce or withdraw (see following section, Withdrawal of benefits) benefits can be\nappealed. In light of a decision of the Federal Constitutional Court of July 2012 on the Asylum Seekers’\nBenefits Act,887 there have been several court decisions concluding that any reduction of benefits would\nbe unconstitutional and therefore inadmissible, but these rulings do not represent the general opinion.888\nThe debate was revived in November 2019 by another decision of the Federal Constitutional Court. In\nthis decision, the Court did not comment on the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act, but made some important\nobservations on the legality of cuts in unemployment benefits and in the social support system in\ngeneral.889 The court argued that temporary sanctions, even to the point of a complete withdrawal of\nbenefits, could be lawful if an unemployed person did not undertake reasonable efforts to overcome the\nneed for support. However, given the extraordinary burden resulting from such sanctions, the court also\nhighlighted that legal provisions which reduce reductions of benefits have to be based on an analysis of\ntheir necessity, suitability and reasonableness. Persons affected by cuts should be able to regain standard\nbenefits once they comply with reasonable obligations. Moreover, individual circumstances must be taken\ninto consideration. Sanctions which are imposed for a fixed period of time and regardless of individual\ncircumstances have to be considered as violating the constitution, according to the Constitutional Court.\n\nAs a result of this decision, the legality of the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act has been questioned again.890\nIn several decisions, the Regional Social Court of Lower Saxony-Bremen has ruled that it is\n‘fundamentally debatable’ whether Section 1a of the Asylum Seekers Benefits Act on the reduction of\nbenefits for certain groups is in line with the constitution’.891 Other courts have also questioned the legality\n\n\n\n\n883\n      Section 1a(1) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n884\n      Joachim Genge, ‘Das geänderte Asylbeweberleistungsgesetz‘ in Informationsverbund Asyl und Migration\n      (ed), Das Migrationspaket: Beilage zum Asylmagazin 8-9/2019, September 2017, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/4aqKg1x, 20.\n885\n      Regional social administration of the Federal State of Berlin, Directive no. 10/2015, 9 December 2015\n      (Rundschreiben Soz Nr. 10/2015 über Umsetzung des AsylbLG in der Fassung des\n      Asylverfahrensbeschleunigungsgesetzes), available at: https://bit.ly/4b9xzse.\n886\n      Section 14(1) and (2) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n887\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Decision 1 BvL 10/10, 1 BvL 2/11, 18 July 2012, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/4awXg5U.\n888\n      Social Court Stade, Decision S 19 AY 19/17 ER, 10 May 2017.\n889\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Decision 1 BvL 7/16, 5 November 2019, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/41tQ7iw.\n890\n      Claudius Voigt, ‘Gesetzlich minimierte Menschenwürde. Therefore, these legal issues have only been raised but have not been decided upon\nby the courts. In any case, issues of constitutionality are a matter for the Federal Constitutional Court and\nso it has to be expected that it will take several years for suitable cases to be discussed at this level. A\nconstitutional complaint about the reduction of benefits under the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act before\nthe June 2019 amendment was rejected by the Federal Constitutional Court on the basis that in the Court\nconsidered the matter of sanctions has already sufficiently decided by prior Court rulings and that in the\nindividual case there was no breach of the Constitution. 893 In the decision of the Federal Constitutional\nCourt of October 2022 (discussed above) the court maintained its earlier rulings, that benefits may be\nmade conditional to obligations to cooperate while emphasising that any distinction between third country\nnationals and German citizens must be reasoned.894 Civil society organisations continue to argue that the\nAsylum Seekers Benefits Act is discriminatory. The debate arose again especially when in May 2022 the\nFederal government decided to award regular social benefits to temporary protection seekers (see Annex\non Temporary Protection) but not to asylum seekers.895\n\nIn practice, the reduction of benefits rarely applies to asylum seekers as long as their asylum procedure\nis ongoing. It may, however, still affect former asylum seekers whose application has been rejected as\n‘manifestly unfounded’ or ‘inadmissible’ (e.g., in cases of Dublin decisions or protection in another EU\ncountry) and in whose cases no emergency legal protection has been granted. For example, the monthly\ncash allowance (‘pocket money’) is often withdrawn or substantially reduced if the person has\n‘absconded’, i.e. failed to be present at the appointment for pick-up by the police for a ‘Dublin transfer’\n(see Dublin: Procedure). In some cases, Social Courts have argued that a reduction of benefits could be\nunlawful as long as no final decision on a possible removal (or transfer to another Dublin state) has been\nmade at the Administrative Court.896 However, such decisions are rare because only a few asylum\nseekers appeal against reductions of benefits upon rejection of their asylum application.\n\nA directive issued in the Federal State of Berlin states that minors are generally exempt from reductions\nof benefits, because the alleged misconduct cannot be held against them (e.g. if their parents have failed\nto provide the authorities with information about their identities).897 However, this policy is exceptional and\nin other Federal States it seems to be commonplace that reductions of benefits are imposed on families\nas a whole, including children.898 The former Federal Government emphasised that children are not\ngenerally exempted from sanctions.899\n\n        3.2. Withdrawal of benefits\n\nHistorically, the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act did not provide for a complete withdrawal of benefits.\nHowever, following the 2019 amendments, foreign nationals who have already been granted international\nprotection in another EU Member State are excluded from all benefits under the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits\nAct.900 Persons affected by this provision will only receive limited benefits for a maximum of two weeks\n\n\n892\n      Social Court Landshut, S 11 AY 79/19 ER, decision of 23 January 2020, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/2BQW41q; Social Court of Berlin, S 50 AY 166/19 ER, 23 December 2019, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/2O691an.\n893\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Case file number 1 BvR 2682/17, Decision of 12 May 2021, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/3GItQ5f.\n894\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Decision 1 BvL 3/21, 19 October 2022, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3z5FN2W, para 65, 75.\n895\n      Refugee Council Bavaria, ‚Menschenwürdige Sozialleistungen für alle sicherstellen!‘, 7 April 2022, available\n      at: http://bit.ly/3J8X1Tk.\n896\n      Regional Social Court Berlin-Brandenburg, Decision L 15 AY 12/17 B ER, 19 July 2017; Social Court\n      Lüneburg, Decision S 26 AY 35/17 ER, 12 September 2017.\n897\n      Regional social administration of the Federal State of Berlin, Directive no. 10/2015, 9 December 2015.\n898\n      Information provided by GGUA, Münster, 19 June 2018.\n899\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 19/26032, 20 January 2021, available in\n      German at: http://bit.ly/3H0dJBw.\n900\n      Section 1(4) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n\n\n                                                      139\n\nand only once every two years (Überbrückungsleistungen). Further benefits may only be provided when\nnecessary ‘in exceptional circumstances’ to avoid particular hardship.901 With Berlin and Rhineland\nPalatinate, at least two Federal States have limited the scope of application of this rule to make sure the\nexclusion does not apply to minors and does not undermine the state obligation to provide a minimal\nsubsistence level of benefits.902\n\nThis exclusion applies to persons whose asylum application in Germany has been finally rejected and\nwhose obligation to leave the territory is enforceable (vollziehbar ausreisepflichtig). This can include\npersons whose appeal against a return decision is pending, if their request for suspensive effect has been\nrejected. The provision does not, however, cover situations in which a removal is impossible in fact or in\nlaw, e.g., if the Member State that has granted protection is not accepting the returnee or if necessary\nidentity documents are missing. In such cases the person affected has to be issued a Duldung and\nremains entitled to benefits under the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n\n      4. Freedom of movement\n\n                                  Indicators: Freedom of Movement\n      1. Is there a mechanism for the dispersal of applicants across the territory of the country?\n                                                                                Yes             No\n\n      2. Does the law provide for restrictions on freedom of movement?           Yes              No\n\n         4.1. Dispersal and geographical restriction\n\nThe freedom of movement of asylum seekers is restricted and they have no right to choose their place of\nresidence. According to the Asylum Act, their right to remain on the territory under a permission to stay\n(Aufenthaltsgestattung) is generally limited to the district of the foreigners’ authority in which the\nresponsible reception centre is located.903 This ‘residence obligation’ (Residenzpflicht), legally called\n‘geographical restriction’ (räumliche Beschränkung), means that asylum seekers are not allowed to leave\nthat area even for short periods of time without permission of the BAMF. However, Federal States have\nthe possibility to extend this geographical restriction to the jurisdiction of other foreigners’ authorities or\nthe area encompassing a whole Federal State, or even to another Federal State, provided that there is\nagreement between the concerned Federal States.904 Asylum seekers in Brandenburg for example have\nthe freedom to move in all of Brandenburg and Berlin.\n\nAs long as the residence obligation applies – i.e. during the initial period of the procedure in most cases\n– the applicant can also request permission to temporary leave the assigned area for urgent public interest\nreasons, where it is necessary for compelling reasons or where refusal of permission would constitute\nundue hardship.905 As a rule, permission shall also be granted if the asylum seeker intends to take up\nemployment or education in another area. Permission shall be granted without delay in cases where the\nperson has appointments with UNHCR or NGOs.906 Next to the residence obligation, freedom of\nmovement is often constrained in practice through the remote location of many reception facilities and the\nlack of accessible public transport (see below, Obligation to stay in initial reception centres). Violation of\nthe residence obligation might have severe consequences. The administrative Court Lower Saxony-\nBreme, for example, has decided in 2023 that asylum seekers who leave the district assigned to them by\n\n\n\n\n901\n        Section 1(4) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n902\n        Der Paritätische Gesamtverband, ‘Arbeitshilfe zum Thema Flucht und Migration. In\nmany cases, even if a revocation procedure was carried out, loss of refugee status would only be a formal\nact, since a foreign national who fulfils all the other requirements for citizenship would usually be entitled\nto stay in Germany and to naturalisation.1420\n\nFees for naturalisation are €255 for an adult person and €51 for children.1421\n\nIn 2022 168,7751422 persons received German citizenship compared to 131,600 in 2021, but available\nstatistics do not differentiate between residence and/or protection statuses.1423 The number of former\nSyrian nationals more than doubled from 2021 to 2022 to 48,320 naturalisations which might stem from\nthe fact that those who fled the Syrian war in 2015 or 2016 now fulfil the criteria of 6 or more years of legal\nstay in Germany. 14,235 Turkish, 6,810 Iraqi and 5,565 Ukrainian nationals have been naturalised. No\nbreakdown of other former nationalities is available for 2022.\n\n       5. Cessation and review of protection status\n\n                                          Indicators: Cessation\n       1. Do beneficiaries have access to free legal assistance at first instance in practice?\n                                                      Yes               With difficulty            No\n\n          5.1    Cessation (Erlöschen) based on individual conduct\n\nWith its entry into force on the 1st January 2023, the grounds for Cessation (Erlöschen) have been\namended in the context of the Act on the Acceleration of asylum court proceedings and asylum procedures\n(see Regular procedure). The aim of the legal reforms was to relieve courts and the Federal Office for\nMigration and Refugees from case overload. Elsewhere the Act has been criticised by civil society\norganisations that the relief for the authorities comes at the price of restricted legal protection for asylum\nseekers and refugees. Concerning grounds for cessation and revocations however, the reforms seem to\nextend the protection of the status, since the grounds for cessation and revocation have been restricted.\nThe Federal government emphasises in the draft of the Act, that the amendments of the cessation grounds\nalso serve to a more coherent and certain legal framework in line with Directive 2011/95/EU and\nrespectively extended the scope of the cessation clause to beneficiaries of subsidiary protection.\nFollowing the reforms, cessation is only possible if the refugee has acted voluntarily. Cessation\n(Erlöschen) of a protection status is defined in Section 72(1) of the Asylum Act as follows:\n\n1418\n         Hailbronner et al., Staatsangehörigkeitsrecht, Beckscher Kurz-Kommentar, 7th Edition, 2022, Section 10\n         Nationality Act, para. 108f.\n1419\n         Section 12 (1)(Nr. 6) Nationality Act.\n1420\n         Hailbronner et al., Staatsangehörigkeitsrecht, Beck’scher Kurz-Kommentar, 7th Edition, 2022, Section 8\n         Nationality Act, para. 103ff.\n1421\n         Section 38 Nationality Act.\n1422\n         Information provided by the BAMF, 10 May 2024.\n1423\n         Federal Statistical Office, 28% mehr Einbürgerungen im Jahr 2022, 30 May 2023, available in German at:\n         https://bit.ly/3uBkPKw.\n\n\n                                                      213\n\nRecognition of constitutional asylum and international protection (including refugees and beneficiaries\nfor subsidiary protection) shall cease to have effect if the foreigner:\n\n       v Unequivocally, voluntarily and in writing declares in front of the Federal Office for Migration and\n         Refugees the renunciation of the status.\n       v has obtained upon his application the German nationality.\n\nAccording to the new Act, the authorities may only start the cessation procedure upon application or\ndeclaration of the refugee. In this case the authorities ask them to hand in the residence permit, travel\ndocuments and other documents relating to the asylum procedure. It is possible to appeal the decision at\nan Administrative Court and the appeal has suspensive effect.1424\n\n          5.2    Revocation (Widerruf) based on change in circumstances\n\nWith the Act on the Acceleration of asylum court proceedings and asylum procedures, the grounds and\nthe procedure for revocations (Widerruf) have been changed drastically. Since the aim of the reforms was\nto relieve the workload of the authorities, the routine revision of the status under the former Section 73\n(2a) Asylum Act has been abandoned completely. Prior to the reforms a revision of the refugee status was\ninitiated automatically by the BAMF three years after the first final decision on the status.1425 Additionally,\nthe grounds for revocation (Widerruf) shall be bound more closely to the concrete events.\n\nMore importantly, the Asylum Act also contains a ‘ceased circumstances’ clause in Section 73(1), and the\nprocedure for the respective loss of status is called revocation (Widerruf) in German. Responsibility for\nthe revocation procedure lies with Department for revocations and cessation at the BAMF.1426 The law\ndistinguishes between revocation grounds for refugees in Section 73 (1) Asylum Act, for beneficiaries of\nsubsidiary protection in Section 73 (2) Asylum Act and revocation grounds for family members of\nbeneficiaries of international protection in Section 73a Asylum Act. The procedure for revocations and\nwithdrawal is now regulated in Section 73b Asylum Act.\n\nAdditionally, for all beneficiaries of international protection (refugees and subsidiary protection holders)\nrevocation is also possible after they have been granted the status, they are found to have committed\noffences which fulfil the criteria of exclusion from refugee status, e.g. acts that violate the aims and\nprinciples of the United Nations or serious criminal offences in Germany (see section on Withdrawal).\n\n       a) Revocation of the refugee status: Section 73(1) Asylum Act\n\nThis provision is generally applicable if the conditions on which the recognition of status was based have\nceased to exist. A cessation of the conditions may especially be assumed in cases where refugees:\n\n       v voluntarily avail themselves of the protection of their country of origin;\n       v after loss of their nationality voluntarily regain the nationality of the country of origin\n       v have obtained another nationality upon application and enjoy the protection of the states from\n         which they obtained the nationality\n       v voluntarily and permanently returned to the country which they left due to former fear of\n         persecution\n       v can no longer refuse to claim the protection of the country of which they are citizens, or if they, as\n         stateless persons, are able to return to the country where they had their usual residence’.1427\n         Accordingly, a
